Hilbert Complexes with Mixed Boundary Conditions Part 3: Biharmonic Complexes Thanks: Acknowledgements. We thank the anonymous referee for careful reading and helpful suggestions to improve the quality of the paper Thanks: Conflict of interest statement. This work does not have any conflicts of interest Thanks: Funding Statement. There are no funders to report for this submission
Dirk Pauly, Michael Schomburg

Abstract
. We show that the biharmonic Hilbert complex with mixed boundary conditions on bounded strong Lipschitz domains is closed and compact. The crucial results are compact embeddings which follow by abstract arguments using functional analysis together with particular regular decompositions. Higher Sobolev order results are also proved. This paper extends recent results on the de Rham and elasticity Hilbert complexes with mixed boundary conditions from [ 3 , 4 ] and results on the biharmonic Hilbert complex with empty or full boundary conditions from [ 6 ] .



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
58Axx, 58Jxx, 35A23, 35Q61

1.
Introduction

In
[
3
]
we investigated the de Rham Hilbert complex
with mixed boundary conditions on bounded strong Lipschitz domains

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sfL^{q-1,2}(\Omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sfL^{q,2}(\Omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sfL^{q+1,2}(\Omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ed^{q-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ed^{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

whose 3D-version for vector proxies reads

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sfL^{2}(\Omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sfL^{2}(\Omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sfL^{2}(\Omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sfL^{2}(\Omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ed^{0}\widehat{=}\grad}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ed^{1}\widehat{=}\rot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ed^{2}\widehat{=}\divergence}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

In
[
4
]
we extended our studies and results to the elasticity complex

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sfL^{2}(\Omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sfL^{2}(\Omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\symGrad}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\RotRot_{\mathbb{S}}^{\!\top}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Div_{\mathbb{S}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

In this contribution, the third part of the series,
we shall investigate the two biharmonic Hilbert complexes with mixed boundary conditions
on a bounded strong Lipschitz domain
\Omega\subset\reals^{3}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sfL^{2}(\Omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sfL^{2}(\Omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gradgrad}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Rot_{\mathbb{S}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Div_{\mathbb{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sfL^{2}(\Omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sfL^{2}(\Omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\devGrad}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\symRot_{\mathbb{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\divDiv_{\mathbb{S}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

Note that these two complexes are formally dual (adjoint) to each other.

As explained in detail in
[
3
,
4
]
,
all these Hilbert complexes share the same geometric structure

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\qquad R(\A_{0})\subset N(\A_{1}),

where
\A_{0}
and
\A_{1}
are densely defined and closed (unbounded) linear operators
between Hilbert spaces
\sfH_{\ell}
.
The corresponding domain Hilbert complex is denoted by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(\A_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(\A_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sfH_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\A_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\A_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

The goal of this article is to show that the previous biharmonic Hilbert complexes are compact,
which is proved by using regular decompositions of the domains of definition
of the respective operators as crucial tool.
We shall follow in close lines the rationale from
[
3
,
4
]
.
Along the way we show the existence of regular potentials and decompositions,
compact embeddings, Helmholtz decompositions,
closed ranges, Friedrichs/Poincaré type estimates,
and bases of the corresponding cohomology groups
(generalised Dirichlet/Neumann tensors).
Due to the similarity of results
we shall only state those which are most important.
In the appendix we will present some of the crucial proofs
which differ from the proofs of the previously investigated complexes.

2.
Biharmonic Complexes I

Throughout this paper, let

\Omega\subset\reals^{3}
be a
bounded strong Lipschitz domain

with boundary
\Gamma
, decomposed into two parts
\Gamma_{\!t}
and
\Gamma_{\!n}:=\Gamma\setminus\overline{\Gamma_{\!t}}

with some
relatively open and strong Lipschitz boundary part

\Gamma_{\!t}\subset\Gamma
.
More precisely, we assume generally that
(\Omega,\Gamma_{\!t})
is a
bounded strong Lipschitz pair
.
We shall consequently use the notations, methods, and results from
our corresponding papers for the de Rham complex
[
3
]
,
for the elasticity complex
[
7
,
4
]
,
and for the biharmonic complexes
[
6
]
.
In particular, we recall
[
3
, Section 2, Section 3]

including the notion of
extendable domains
.
The standard Lebesgue and Sobolev spaces (scalar or tensor valued)
are denoted by
\sfL^{2}(\Omega)
and
\sfH^{k}(\Omega)
with
k\in\nat_{0}
.

We recall that weak and strong boundary conditions coincide
for the standard Sobolev spaces with mixed boundary conditions, i.e.,

(1)

\displaystyle\mbH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)=\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega),

cf.
[
3
, Lemma 3.2, Theorem 4.6]
.
Below we shall show that “
strong
=
weak
” holds generally also
for the biharmonic complex.
Note that
\sfH^{k}_{\emptyset}(\Omega)=\sfH^{k}(\Omega)
and
\sfH^{0}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)=\sfL^{2}(\Omega)
.

We introduce as usual
\Grad
,
\Rot
, and
\Div
as ‘row-wise’ incarnations
of the classical operators
\grad
,
\rot
, and
\divergence

from the de Rham complex.

2.1.
Operators

Let

\Gradgrad
,
\Rot
,
\Div
,
\devGrad
,
\symRot
, and
\divDiv

be realised as densely defined (unbounded) linear operators

\displaystyle\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\mathring{\Gradgrad}}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}:D(\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\mathring{\Gradgrad}}_{\Gamma_{\!t}})\subset\sfL^{2}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\to\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega);

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle\mapsto\Gradgrad u,

\displaystyle\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\mathring{\Rot}}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}:D(\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\mathring{\Rot}}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}})\subset\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\to\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Omega);

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle\mapsto\Rot S,

\displaystyle\mathring{\Div}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}:D(\mathring{\Div}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}})\subset\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\to\sfL^{2}(\Omega);

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto\Div T,

\displaystyle\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\mathring{\Grad}}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}:D(\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\mathring{\Grad}}_{\Gamma_{\!t}})\subset\sfL^{2}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\to\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Omega);

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\mapsto\devGrad v,

\displaystyle\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\mathring{\Rot}}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}:D(\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\mathring{\Rot}}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}})\subset\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\to\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega);

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto\symRot T,

\displaystyle\mathring{\divDiv}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}:D(\mathring{\divDiv}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}})\subset\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\to\sfL^{2}(\Omega);

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle\mapsto\divDiv S,

where

\sym S:=\frac{1}{2}(S+S^{\top})
and

\dev T:=T-\frac{1}{3}(\tr T)\id
,
with domains of definition

\displaystyle D(\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\mathring{\Gradgrad}}_{\Gamma_{\!t}})

\displaystyle:=\sfC^{\infty}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega),

\displaystyle D(\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\mathring{\Rot}}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}})

\displaystyle:=\sfC^{\infty}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega),

\displaystyle D(\mathring{\Div}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}})

\displaystyle:=\sfC^{\infty}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega),

\displaystyle D(\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\mathring{\Grad}}_{\Gamma_{\!t}})

\displaystyle:=\sfC^{\infty}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega),

\displaystyle D(\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\mathring{\Rot}}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}})

\displaystyle:=\sfC^{\infty}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega),

\displaystyle D(\mathring{\divDiv}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}})

\displaystyle:=\sfC^{\infty}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega),

satisfying the complex properties

\displaystyle\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\mathring{\Rot}}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}\,\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\mathring{\Gradgrad}}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}

\displaystyle\subset 0,

\displaystyle\mathring{\Div}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}\,\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\mathring{\Rot}}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}\subset 0,

\displaystyle\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\mathring{\Rot}}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}\,\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\mathring{\Grad}}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}

\displaystyle\subset 0,

\displaystyle\mathring{\divDiv}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}\,\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\mathring{\Rot}}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}\subset 0.

For elementary properties of these operators see, e.g.,
[
7
]
,
in particular, we have a collection of formulas presented in Lemma
A.1
.
Here, we introduce the Lebesgue Hilbert spaces
and the test spaces of symmetric and deviatoric tensor fields

\displaystyle\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{S\in\sfL^{2}(\Omega):\skw S=0\big\},

\displaystyle\sfC^{\infty}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\sfC^{\infty}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\cap\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega),

\displaystyle\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{S\in\sfL^{2}(\Omega):\tr T=0\big\},

\displaystyle\sfC^{\infty}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\sfC^{\infty}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\cap\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Omega),

respectively. We get the first and second biharmonic complexes on smooth tensor fields

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sfL^{2}(\Omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sfL^{2}(\Omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\mathring{\Gradgrad}}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\mathring{\Rot}}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathring{\Div}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sfL^{2}(\Omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sfL^{2}(\Omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\mathring{\Grad}}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\mathring{\Rot}}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathring{\divDiv}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

For a more algebraically structured introduction of the latter operators
suggested by Rainer Picard see Appendix
B
.
The closures

\displaystyle\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\Gradgrad}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}

\displaystyle:=\overline{\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\mathring{\Gradgrad}}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}},

\displaystyle\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\Rot}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}

\displaystyle:=\overline{\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\mathring{\Rot}}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}},

\displaystyle\Div_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}

\displaystyle:=\overline{\mathring{\Div}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}},

\displaystyle\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\Grad}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}

\displaystyle:=\overline{\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\mathring{\Grad}}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}},

\displaystyle\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\Rot}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}

\displaystyle:=\overline{\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\mathring{\Rot}}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}},

\displaystyle\divDiv_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}

\displaystyle:=\overline{\mathring{\divDiv}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}}

and Hilbert space adjoints
are given by the densely defined and closed linear operators

\displaystyle\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\Gradgrad}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}:D(\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\Gradgrad}_{\Gamma_{\!t}})\subset\sfL^{2}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\to\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega);

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle\mapsto\Gradgrad u,

\displaystyle\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\Gradgrad}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}^{*}=\bdivDiv_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!n}}:D(\bdivDiv_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!n}})\subset\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\to\sfL^{2}(\Omega);

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle\mapsto\divDiv S,

\displaystyle\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\Rot}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}:D(\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\Rot}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}})\subset\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\to\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Omega);

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle\mapsto\Rot S,

\displaystyle\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\Rot}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}^{*}=\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\bRot}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!n}}:D(\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\bRot}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!n}})\subset\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\to\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega);

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto\symRot T,

\displaystyle\Div_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}:D(\Div_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}})\subset\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\to\sfL^{2}(\Omega);

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto\Div T,

\displaystyle\Div_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}^{*}=-\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\bGrad}_{\Gamma_{\!n}}:D(\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\bGrad}_{\Gamma_{\!n}})\subset\sfL^{2}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\to\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Omega);

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\mapsto-\devGrad v,

\displaystyle\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\Grad}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}:D(\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\Grad}_{\Gamma_{\!t}})\subset\sfL^{2}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\to\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Omega);

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\mapsto\devGrad v,

\displaystyle\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\Grad}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}^{*}=-\bDiv_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!n}}:D(\bDiv_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!n}})\subset\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\to\sfL^{2}(\Omega);

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto-\Div T,

\displaystyle\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\Rot}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}:D(\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\Rot}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}})\subset\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\to\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega);

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto\symRot T,

\displaystyle\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\Rot}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}^{*}=\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\bRot}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!n}}:D(\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\bRot}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!n}})\subset\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\to\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Omega);

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle\mapsto\Rot S,

\displaystyle\divDiv_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}:D(\divDiv_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}})\subset\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\to\sfL^{2}(\Omega);

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle\mapsto\divDiv S,

\displaystyle\divDiv_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}^{*}=\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\bGradgrad}_{\Gamma_{\!n}}:D(\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\bGradgrad}_{\Gamma_{\!n}})\subset\sfL^{2}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\to\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega);

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle\mapsto\Gradgrad u,

with domains of definition

\displaystyle D(\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\Gradgrad}_{\Gamma_{\!t}})

\displaystyle=\sfH_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Gradgrad,\Omega),

\displaystyle D(\bdivDiv_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!n}})

\displaystyle=\mbH_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!n}}(\divDiv,\Omega),

\displaystyle D(\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\Rot}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}})

\displaystyle=\sfH_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Rot,\Omega),

\displaystyle D(\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\bRot}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!n}})

\displaystyle=\mbH_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!n}}(\symRot,\Omega),

\displaystyle D(\Div_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}})

\displaystyle=\sfH_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Div,\Omega),

\displaystyle D(\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\bGrad}_{\Gamma_{\!n}})

\displaystyle=\mbH_{\Gamma_{\!n}}(\devGrad,\Omega),

\displaystyle D(\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\Grad}_{\Gamma_{\!t}})

\displaystyle=\sfH_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\devGrad,\Omega),

\displaystyle D(\bDiv_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!n}})

\displaystyle=\mbH_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!n}}(\Div,\Omega),

\displaystyle D(\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\Rot}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}})

\displaystyle=\sfH_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\symRot,\Omega),

\displaystyle D(\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\bRot}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!n}})

\displaystyle=\mbH_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!n}}(\Rot,\Omega),

\displaystyle D(\divDiv_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}})

\displaystyle=\sfH_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\divDiv,\Omega),

\displaystyle D(\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\bGradgrad}_{\Gamma_{\!n}})

\displaystyle=\mbH_{\Gamma_{\!n}}(\Gradgrad,\Omega).

We shall introduce the latter Sobolev spaces in the next section.

2.2.
Sobolev Spaces

Let

\displaystyle\sfH(\Gradgrad,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{u\in\sfL^{2}(\Omega):\Gradgrad u\in\sfL^{2}(\Omega)\big\},

\displaystyle\sfH_{\mathbb{S}}(\Rot,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{S\in\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega):\Rot S\in\sfL^{2}(\Omega)\big\},

\displaystyle\sfH_{\mathbb{T}}(\Div,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{T\in\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Omega):\Div T\in\sfL^{2}(\Omega)\big\},

\displaystyle\sfH(\devGrad,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{v\in\sfL^{2}(\Omega):\devGrad v\in\sfL^{2}(\Omega)\big\},

\displaystyle\sfH_{\mathbb{T}}(\symRot,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{T\in\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Omega):\symRot T\in\sfL^{2}(\Omega)\big\},

\displaystyle\sfH_{\mathbb{S}}(\divDiv,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{S\in\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega):\divDiv S\in\sfL^{2}(\Omega)\big\}.

Note that
S\in\sfH_{\mathbb{S}}(\Rot,\Omega)
implies
\Rot S\in\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Omega)
,
cf. Lemma
A.1
,
and that we have by Nečas’ inequality
and a Korn type inequality for
\dev
the regularities

(2)

\displaystyle\sfH(\Gradgrad,\Omega)=\sfH^{2}(\Omega),\qquad\sfH(\devGrad,\Omega)=\sfH^{1}(\Omega)

with equivalent norms, see, e.g.,

[
5
, Lemma 8.2]
and
[
6
, Lemma 3.2]
.
Moreover, we define boundary conditions in the
strong sense

as closures of respective test fields, i.e.,

\displaystyle\sfH_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Gradgrad,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\overline{\sfC^{\infty}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)}^{\sfH^{2}(\Omega)}=\sfH^{2}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega),

\displaystyle\sfH_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Rot,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\overline{\sfC^{\infty}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)}^{\sfH_{\mathbb{S}}(\Rot,\Omega)},

\displaystyle\sfH_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Div,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\overline{\sfC^{\infty}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)}^{\sfH_{\mathbb{T}}(\Div,\Omega)},

\displaystyle\sfH_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\devGrad,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\overline{\sfC^{\infty}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)}^{\sfH^{1}(\Omega)}=\sfH^{1}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega),

\displaystyle\sfH_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\symRot,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\overline{\sfC^{\infty}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)}^{\sfH_{\mathbb{T}}(\symRot,\Omega)},

\displaystyle\sfH_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\divDiv,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\overline{\sfC^{\infty}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)}^{\sfH_{\mathbb{S}}(\divDiv,\Omega)}.

For
\Gamma_{\!t}=\emptyset
we may skip the index
\emptyset

which is justified by density.
Spaces with vanishing differential operator coincide with kernels
and are denoted by an additional index
0

at the lower right corner, e.g.,

\sfH_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t},0}(\Rot,\Omega)=N(\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\Rot}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}),\qquad\sfH_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t},0}(\Div,\Omega)=N(\Div_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}).

We need also the Sobolev spaces with boundary conditions defined
in the
weak sense
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mbH_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Gradgrad,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{u\in\sfH^{2}(\Omega):\langle\Gradgrad u,\Phi\rangle_{\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega)}=\langle u,\divDiv\Phi\rangle_{\sfL^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\forall\,\Phi\in\sfC^{\infty}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!n}}(\Omega)\big\},

\displaystyle\mbH_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Rot,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{S\in\sfH_{\mathbb{S}}(\Rot,\Omega):\langle\Rot S,\Psi\rangle_{\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Omega)}=\langle S,\symRot\Psi\rangle_{\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\forall\,\Psi\in\sfC^{\infty}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!n}}(\Omega)\big\},

\displaystyle\mbH_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Div,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{T\in\sfH_{\mathbb{T}}(\Div,\Omega):\langle\Div T,\phi\rangle_{\sfL^{2}(\Omega)}=-\langle T,\devGrad\phi\rangle_{\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\forall\,\phi\in\sfC^{\infty}_{\Gamma_{\!n}}(\Omega)\big\},

\displaystyle\mbH_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\devGrad,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{v\in\sfH^{1}(\Omega):\langle\devGrad v,\Psi\rangle_{\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Omega)}=-\langle v,\Div\Psi\rangle_{\sfL^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\forall\,\Psi\in\sfC^{\infty}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!n}}(\Omega)\big\},

\displaystyle\mbH_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\symRot,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{T\in\sfH_{\mathbb{T}}(\symRot,\Omega):\langle\symRot T,\Phi\rangle_{\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega)}=\langle T,\Rot\Phi\rangle_{\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\forall\,\Phi\in\sfC^{\infty}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!n}}(\Omega)\big\},

\displaystyle\mbH_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\divDiv,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{S\in\sfH_{\mathbb{S}}(\divDiv,\Omega):\langle\divDiv S,\phi\rangle_{\sfL^{2}(\Omega)}=\langle S,\Gradgrad\phi\rangle_{\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\forall\,\phi\in\sfC^{\infty}_{\Gamma_{\!n}}(\Omega)\big\}.

Note that “
strong
\subset
weak
” holds, i.e.,

\sfH_{\cdots}(\cdots,\Omega)\subset\mbH_{\cdots}(\cdots,\Omega)
, e.g.,

\sfH_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Rot,\Omega)\subset\mbH_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Rot,\Omega),\qquad\sfH_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Div,\Omega)\subset\mbH_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Div,\Omega),

and that the complex properties hold in both the strong and the weak case, e.g.,

\devGrad\sfH_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\subset\sfH_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t},0}(\symRot,\Omega),\qquad\Rot\mbH_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Rot,\Omega)\subset\mbH_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t},0}(\Div,\Omega),

which follows immediately by the definitions.
In Remark
2.3
below we comment on the question
whether “
strong
=
weak
” holds in general.

2.3.
Higher Order Sobolev Spaces

For
k\in\nat_{0}
we define higher order Sobolev spaces by

\displaystyle\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\sfH^{k}(\Omega)\cap\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega),

\displaystyle\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\sfH^{k}(\Omega)\cap\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Omega),

\displaystyle\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\overline{\sfC^{\infty}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)}^{\sfH^{k}(\Omega)}=\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\cap\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega),

\displaystyle\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\overline{\sfC^{\infty}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)}^{\sfH^{k}(\Omega)}=\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\cap\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Omega),

\displaystyle\sfH^{k}(\Gradgrad,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{u\in\sfH^{k}(\Omega):\Gradgrad u\in\sfH^{k}(\Omega)\big\},

\displaystyle\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Gradgrad,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{u\in\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\cap\sfH_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Gradgrad,\Omega):\Gradgrad u\in\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\big\},

\displaystyle\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Rot,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{S\in\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega):\Rot S\in\sfH^{k}(\Omega)\big\},

\displaystyle\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Rot,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{S\in\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\cap\sfH_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Rot,\Omega):\Rot S\in\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\big\},

\displaystyle\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Div,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{T\in\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Omega):\Div T\in\sfH^{k}(\Omega)\big\},

\displaystyle\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Div,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{T\in\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\cap\sfH_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Div,\Omega):\Div T\in\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\big\},

\displaystyle\sfH^{k}(\devGrad,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{v\in\sfH^{k}(\Omega):\devGrad v\in\sfH^{k}(\Omega)\big\},

\displaystyle\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\devGrad,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{v\in\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\cap\sfH_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\devGrad,\Omega):\devGrad v\in\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\big\},

\displaystyle\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{T}}(\symRot,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{T\in\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{T}}(\Omega):\symRot T\in\sfH^{k}(\Omega)\big\},

\displaystyle\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\symRot,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{T\in\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\cap\sfH_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\symRot,\Omega):\symRot T\in\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\big\},

\displaystyle\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S}}(\divDiv,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{S\in\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega):\divDiv S\in\sfH^{k}(\Omega)\big\},

\displaystyle\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\divDiv,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{S\in\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\cap\sfH_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\divDiv,\Omega):\divDiv S\in\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\big\}.

For the first reading we recommend to only regard the case
k=0

from Section
2.2
.

Note that, e.g., for the latter
\divDiv
-Sobolev spaces,
we have
\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\emptyset}(\divDiv,\Omega)=\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S}}(\divDiv,\Omega)

and
\sfH^{0}_{\mathbb{S},\emptyset}(\divDiv,\Omega)=\sfH_{\mathbb{S}}(\divDiv,\Omega)

as well as
\sfH^{0}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\divDiv,\Omega)=\sfH_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\divDiv,\Omega)
.
For
\Gamma_{\!t}\neq\emptyset
it holds

\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\divDiv,\Omega)=\big\{S\in\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega):\divDiv S\in\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\big\},\qquad k\geq 2,

but for
\Gamma_{\!t}\neq\emptyset
and
k=0
and
k=1

\displaystyle\sfH^{0}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\divDiv,\Omega)

\displaystyle=\sfH_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\divDiv,\Omega)

\displaystyle\subsetneq\big\{S\in\underbrace{\sfH^{0}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)}_{=\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega)}:\divDiv S\in\underbrace{\sfH^{0}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)}_{=\sfL^{2}(\Omega)}\big\}=\sfH_{\mathbb{S}}(\divDiv,\Omega),

\displaystyle\sfH^{1}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\divDiv,\Omega)

\displaystyle\subsetneq\big\{S\in\sfH^{1}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega):\divDiv S\in\sfH^{1}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\big\},

respectively. As before, we introduce the kernels

\displaystyle\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t},0}(\divDiv,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\cap\sfH_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t},0}(\divDiv,\Omega)=\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\divDiv,\Omega)\cap\sfH_{\mathbb{S},0}(\divDiv,\Omega)

\displaystyle\;=\big\{S\in\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\divDiv,\Omega):\divDiv S=0\big\}.

The corresponding remarks and definitions extend also
to the
\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Gradgrad,\Omega)
,
\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Rot,\Omega)
,
\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Div,\Omega)
,

\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\devGrad,\Omega)
, and
\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\symRot,\Omega)
-spaces.
In particular, we have for
\Gamma_{\!t}\neq\emptyset
and
k\geq 1
and, e.g.,

\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Rot,\Omega)
, the observations

\displaystyle\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Rot,\Omega)

\displaystyle=\big\{S\in\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega):\Rot S\in\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\big\},

\displaystyle\sfH^{0}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Rot,\Omega)

\displaystyle=\sfH_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Rot,\Omega)\subsetneq\big\{S\in\underbrace{\sfH^{0}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)}_{=\sfL^{2}_{\mathbb{S}}(\Omega)}:\Rot S\in\underbrace{\sfH^{0}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)}_{=\sfL^{2}(\Omega)}\big\}=\sfH_{\mathbb{S}}(\Rot,\Omega),

\displaystyle\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t},0}(\Rot,\Omega)

\displaystyle=\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\cap\sfH_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t},0}(\Rot,\Omega)=\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Rot,\Omega)\cap\sfH_{\mathbb{S},0}(\Rot,\Omega)

\displaystyle=\big\{S\in\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Rot,\Omega):\Rot S=0\big\}.

Analogously, we define the Sobolev spaces

\mbH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Gradgrad,\Omega)
,
\mbH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Rot,\Omega)
,
\mbH^{k}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Div,\Omega)
,

\mbH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\devGrad,\Omega)
,
\mbH^{k}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\symRot,\Omega)
, and
\mbH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\divDiv,\Omega)

using the respective Sobolev spaces with weak boundary conditions
\mbH^{\cdots}_{\cdots}(\cdots,\Omega)

in the definitions, e.g.,

\displaystyle\mbH^{k}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\symRot,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{T\in\mbH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\cap\mbH_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\symRot,\Omega):\symRot T\in\mbH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\big\}

\displaystyle\;=\big\{T\in\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\cap\mbH_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\symRot,\Omega):\symRot T\in\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\big\},

where we have used (
1
).
Note that again “
strong
\subset
weak
” holds, i.e.,

\sfH^{\cdots}_{\cdots}(\cdots,\Omega)\subset\mbH^{\cdots}_{\cdots}(\cdots,\Omega)
, e.g.,

\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Rot,\Omega)\subset\mbH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Rot,\Omega)
,
and that the complex properties hold in both the strong and the weak case, e.g.,

\Gradgrad\sfH^{k+2}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\subset\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t},0}(\Rot,\Omega),\qquad\symRot\mbH^{k}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\symRot,\Omega)\subset\mbH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t},0}(\divDiv,\Omega).

In the forthcoming sections we shall also investigate
whether indeed “
strong
=
weak
” holds.
We start with a simple implication from (
1
).

Corollary 2.1
.

\mbH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)=\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)
and

\mbH^{k}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)=\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)
, i.e.,
weak and strong boundary conditions coincide
for the standard Sobolev spaces of symmetric and deviatoric tensor fields
with mixed boundary conditions, respectively.

As in (
2
) and with Corollary
2.1

we get the following.

Lemma 2.2

(higher order weak and strong partial boundary conditions coincide)
.

(i)

For
k\geq 0
it holds

\displaystyle\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\devGrad,\Omega)

\displaystyle=\sfH^{k+1}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)=\mbH^{k+1}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega),

\displaystyle\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Gradgrad,\Omega)

\displaystyle=\sfH^{k+2}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)=\mbH^{k+2}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega).

(ii)

For
k\geq 1
it holds

\displaystyle\mbH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\devGrad,\Omega)

\displaystyle=\big\{v\in\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega):\devGrad v\in\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\big\}=\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\devGrad,\Omega)=\sfH^{k+1}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega),

\displaystyle\mbH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Rot,\Omega)

\displaystyle=\big\{S\in\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega):\Rot S\in\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\big\}=\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Rot,\Omega),

\displaystyle\mbH^{k}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\symRot,\Omega)

\displaystyle=\big\{T\in\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega):\symRot T\in\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\big\}=\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\symRot,\Omega),

\displaystyle\mbH^{k}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Div,\Omega)

\displaystyle=\big\{T\in\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega):\Div T\in\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\big\}=\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Div,\Omega).

(iii)

For
k\geq 2
it holds

\displaystyle\mbH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Gradgrad,\Omega)

\displaystyle=\big\{u\in\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega):\Gradgrad u\in\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\big\}=\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Gradgrad,\Omega),

\displaystyle\mbH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\divDiv,\Omega)

\displaystyle=\big\{S\in\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega):\divDiv S\in\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\big\}=\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\divDiv,\Omega).

Remark 2.3

(weak and strong partial boundary conditions coincide)
.

In
[
6
,
5
]
we could prove the corresponding results “
strong
=
weak
”
for the whole two biharmonic complexes
but only with empty or full boundary conditions (
\Gamma_{t}=\emptyset
or
\Gamma_{t}=\Gamma
).
Therefore, in these special cases, the adjoints are well-defined on the spaces with strong boundary conditions as well.

Lemma
2.2
shows that
for higher values of
k
indeed “
strong
=
weak
” holds.
Thus to show “
strong
=
weak
” in general
we only have to prove that equality holds in the remains cases

k=0
and
k=1
, i.e.,
we only have to show

\displaystyle\mbH_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\devGrad,\Omega)

\displaystyle\subset\sfH_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\devGrad,\Omega),

\displaystyle\mbH_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Gradgrad,\Omega)

\displaystyle\subset\sfH_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Gradgrad,\Omega),

\displaystyle\mbH_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Div,\Omega)

\displaystyle\subset\sfH_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Div,\Omega),

\displaystyle\mbH^{1}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Gradgrad,\Omega)

\displaystyle\subset\sfH^{1}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Gradgrad,\Omega),

\displaystyle\mbH_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Rot,\Omega)

\displaystyle\subset\sfH_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Rot,\Omega),

\displaystyle\mbH_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\divDiv,\Omega)

\displaystyle\subset\sfH_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\divDiv,\Omega),

\displaystyle\mbH_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\symRot,\Omega)

\displaystyle\subset\sfH_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\symRot,\Omega),

\displaystyle\mbH^{1}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\divDiv,\Omega)

\displaystyle\subset\sfH^{1}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\divDiv,\Omega).

The most delicate situation appears due to the second order nature of
\divDiv_{\mathbb{S}}
.
In Corollary
3.11
we shall show
using regular decompositions that these results
(weak and strong boundary conditions coincide for the biharmonic complexes for all
k\geq 0
)
indeed hold true.

2.4.
More Sobolev Spaces

For
k\in\nat
we introduce also slightly less regular higher order Sobolev spaces by

\displaystyle\sfH^{k,k-1}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Gradgrad,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{u\in\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\cap\sfH_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Gradgrad,\Omega):\Gradgrad u\in\sfH^{k-1}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\big\},

\displaystyle\mbH^{k,k-1}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Gradgrad,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{u\in\mbH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\cap\mbH_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Gradgrad,\Omega):\Gradgrad u\in\mbH^{k-1}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\big\},

\displaystyle\sfH^{k,k-1}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\divDiv,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{S\in\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\cap\sfH_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\divDiv,\Omega):\divDiv S\in\sfH^{k-1}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\big\},

\displaystyle\mbH^{k,k-1}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\divDiv,\Omega)

\displaystyle:=\big\{S\in\mbH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\cap\mbH_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\divDiv,\Omega):\divDiv S\in\mbH^{k-1}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\big\},

and we extend all conventions of our notations.
These spaces can be ignored at the first reading.

We have for the kernels of
\divDiv_{\mathbb{S}}

\displaystyle\sfH^{k,k-1}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t},0}(\divDiv,\Omega)

\displaystyle=\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t},0}(\divDiv,\Omega),

\displaystyle\mbH^{k,k-1}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t},0}(\divDiv,\Omega)

\displaystyle=\mbH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t},0}(\divDiv,\Omega),

and by Nečas’ inequality, cf. (
2
),

\displaystyle\sfH^{k,k-1}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Gradgrad,\Omega)

\displaystyle=\sfH^{k+1}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)=\mbH^{k+1}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\subset\mbH^{k,k-1}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Gradgrad,\Omega).

The intersection with
\sfH_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Gradgrad,\Omega)
,
\mbH_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Gradgrad,\Omega)

and
\sfH_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\divDiv,\Omega)
,
\mbH_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\divDiv,\Omega)
, respectively,
is only needed if
k=1
.
As before, we observe

\sfH^{k,k-1}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\divDiv,\Omega)\subset\mbH^{k,k-1}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\divDiv,\Omega)
,
i.e., “
strong
\subset
weak
”,
and in both cases (weak and strong) the complex properties hold, e.g.,

\Gradgrad\sfH^{k,k-1}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Gradgrad,\Omega)\subset\sfH^{k-1}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t},0}(\Rot,\Omega)
.

Similar to Lemma
2.2
we have the following.

Lemma 2.4

(higher order weak and strong partial boundary conditions coincide)
.

For
k\geq 2

\displaystyle\mbH^{k,k-1}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Gradgrad,\Omega)

\displaystyle=\big\{u\in\sfH^{k}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega):\Gradgrad u\in\sfH^{k-1}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\big\}=\sfH^{k,k-1}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Gradgrad,\Omega)=\sfH^{k+1}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega),

\displaystyle\mbH^{k,k-1}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\divDiv,\Omega)

\displaystyle=\big\{S\in\sfH^{k}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega):\divDiv S\in\sfH^{k-1}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega)\big\}=\sfH^{k,k-1}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}(\divDiv,\Omega).

2.5.
Some Biharmonic Complexes

By definition we have densely defined and closed (unbounded) linear operators
defining six dual pairs

\displaystyle(\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\Gradgrad}_{\Gamma_{\!t}},\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\Gradgrad}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}^{*})

\displaystyle=(\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\Gradgrad}_{\Gamma_{\!t}},\bdivDiv_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!n}}),

\displaystyle(\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\Rot}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}},\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\Rot}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}^{*})

\displaystyle=(\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\Rot}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}},\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\bRot}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!n}}),

\displaystyle(\Div_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}},\Div_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}^{*})

\displaystyle=(\Div_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}},-\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\bGrad}_{\Gamma_{\!n}}),

\displaystyle(\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\Grad}_{\Gamma_{\!t}},\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\Grad}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}^{*})

\displaystyle=(\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\Grad}_{\Gamma_{\!t}},-\bDiv_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!n}}),

\displaystyle(\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\Rot}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}},\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\Rot}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}^{*})

\displaystyle=(\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\Rot}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}},\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\bRot}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!n}}),

\displaystyle(\divDiv_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}},\divDiv_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}^{*})

\displaystyle=(\divDiv_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}},\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\bGradgrad}_{\Gamma_{\!n}}).

[
3
, Remark 2.5, Remark 2.6]
show the complex properties

\displaystyle\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\Rot}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\Gradgrad}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}

\displaystyle\subset 0,

\displaystyle\Div_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\Rot}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}

\displaystyle\subset 0,

\displaystyle\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\Rot}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\Grad}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}

\displaystyle\subset 0,

\displaystyle\divDiv_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!t}}\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\Rot}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}

\displaystyle\subset 0,

\displaystyle\bdivDiv_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!n}}\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\bRot}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!n}}

\displaystyle\subset 0,

\displaystyle-\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\bRot}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!n}}\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\bGrad}_{\Gamma_{\!n}}

\displaystyle\subset 0,

\displaystyle-\bDiv_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!n}}\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\bRot}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!n}}

\displaystyle\subset 0,

\displaystyle\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\bRot}_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!n}}\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\bGradgrad}_{\Gamma_{\!n}}

\displaystyle\subset 0.

Hence we get the two primal and dual biharmonic Hilbert complexes

(3)

The long primal and dual biharmonic Hilbert complexes,
cf.
[

, (12)]
, read

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\RT_{\Gamma_{\!t}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sfL^{2}(\Omega)}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\Rot}_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!t}}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\Gamma_{\!n}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\Gamma_{\!n}}}}

with the additional complex properties

\displaystyle R(\iota_{\RT_{\Gamma_{\!t}}})

\displaystyle=N(\prescript{}{\mathbb{T}}{\Grad}_{\Gamma_{\!t}})=\RT_{\Gamma_{\!t}},

\displaystyle\overline{R(\Div_{\mathbb{T},\Gamma_{\!n}})}

\displaystyle=\RT_{\Gamma_{\!t}}^{\bot_{\sfL^{2}(\Omega)}},

\displaystyle R(\iota_{\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}})

\displaystyle=N(\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\Gradgrad}_{\Gamma_{\!t}})=\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\Gamma_{\!t}},

\displaystyle\overline{R(\divDiv_{\mathbb{S},\Gamma_{\!n}})}

\displaystyle=(\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\Gamma_{\!t}})^{\bot_{\sfL^{2}(\Omega)}},

where

\displaystyle\RT_{\Sigma}=\begin{cases}\{0\}&\text{if }\Sigma\neq\emptyset,\\
\RT&\text{if }\Sigma=\emptyset,\end{cases}\qquad\text{with}\qquad\RT:=\big\{\reals^{3}\ni x\mapsto ax+q:a\in\reals,\,q\in\reals^{3}\big\},

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\Sigma}=\begin{cases}\{0\}&\text{if }\Sigma\neq\emptyset,\\
\mathbb{P}^{1}&\text{if }\Sigma=\emptyset,\end{cases}\qquad\text{with}\qquad\mathbb{P}^{1}:=\big\{\reals^{3}\ni x\mapsto q\cdot x+a:a\in\reals,\,q\in\reals^{3}\big\}

denote the global Raviart-Thomas fields
and the global polynomials of degree less or equal to
1
in
\Omega
, respectively.
We have
\dim\RT=\dim\mathbb{P}^{1}=4
.
Note that, e.g., by Lemma
2.2
(i) it holds

N(\prescript{}{\mathbb{S}}{\Gradgrad}_{\Gamma_{\!t}})=\big\{u\in\sfH^{2}_{\Gamma_{\!t}}(\Omega):\Gradgrad u=0\big\}.
